### Algebraic decomposition and topological fibrations of the $U(4) \to SU(2)$ quotient space
Source: arXiv
Published: 2026-10-20 04:00 UTC
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23859
Source meta: preprint · primary · R4 · P5 · signal

arXiv:2610.23859v1 Announce Type: new Abstract: We investigate the algebraic and topological properties of the unitary group $U(4)$ subjected to a non-standard continuous symmetry breaking cascade down to an $SU(2)_H$ subgroup. By explicitly embedding the unbroken $SU(2)_H$ symmetry into the lower-right quadrant of the fundamental matrix representation, we demonstrate that the resulting 12-dimensional quotient space, $SU(4)/SU(2)_H$, undergoes a strictly asymmetric geometric partition. This specific breaking pattern uniquely isolates an 8-dimensional sector that retains closed commutation relations, forming a rigorous $\mathfrak{su}(3)$ Lie subalgebra. Concurrently, it isolates a 4-dimensional off-diagonal sector that fails to close algebraically, generating a purely non-linear topological coset. By analyzing the global spherical fibrations of the manifold, we show that this non-linear territory reduces to a $\mathbb{C}P^1 \cong S^2$ base space.

### Foundations of Wilson lines and loops from basic concepts to gauge field reconstruction
Source: arXiv
Published: 2026-10-20 04:00 UTC
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23897
Source meta: preprint · primary · R4 · P5 · signal

arXiv:2610.23897v1 Announce Type: new Abstract: This review presents a concise and unified treatment of Wilson lines and Wilson loops in continuum gauge theory, emphasizing their interpretation as parallel transport and their status as gauge covariant transporters and gauge invariant loop observables. Starting from the problem of comparing charged fields at separated points, we derive the path ordered exponential formulation and collect the key kinematical identities: gauge covariance, composition under path concatenation, inversion, and base point dependence. We then explain how local information is encoded in suitable small segment and small loop limits, and how these limits enter reconstruction questions for the underlying gauge connection. Seen as an inverse problem, this perspective clarifies how much of the gauge connection is encoded in loop observables and why traced Wilson loops, while gauge invariant, are intrinsically lossy without additional input.

### Horizon-Boundary Localization of the Hawking Temperature in a Weak AdS/CFT Extension
Source: arXiv
Published: 2026-10-20 04:00 UTC
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23916
Source meta: preprint · primary · R4 · P5 · signal

arXiv:2610.23916v1 Announce Type: new Abstract: We establish a conditional boundary-localization statement for the Hawking temperature. For a static, Euclideanizable black hole with a non-degenerate horizon, the temperature is determined entirely by the first non-vanishing near-horizon data. This result is obtained consistently through three approaches: removal of the Euclidean conical defect, the horizon boundary term in the two-dimensional Gauss-Bonnet theorem, and a complex residue calculation. In asymptotically anti-de Sitter spacetime, the Euclidean time periodicity selected at the horizon extends to the conformal boundary, so the boundary conformal field theory inherits the same thermal period up to time normalization. This provides only a weak, temperature-sector extension of the AdS/CFT framework and does not imply a full duality. For dynamical spherical spacetimes, the Hamilton-Jacobi analysis selects the trapping or apparent horizon as the local thermal boundary, whereas a separated event horizon is generally not associated with the relevant pole.

### Finite quantum gravity in locally conformally flat spacetime through covariant spectral regularization
Source: arXiv
Published: 2026-10-20 04:00 UTC
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23924
Source meta: preprint · primary · R4 · P5 · signal

arXiv:2610.23924v1 Announce Type: new Abstract: Pure gravity quantum gravity (pure gravity QG) is non-renormalizable at two and higher loop level. One of the main characteristics of objects occurring in QG is their Lorentz covariance. QG necessitates the analysis of a collection of Lorentz covariant terms of higher and higher order, which is not feasible using renormailization. This paper describes how the regularization technique known as covariant spectral regularization (CSR) can be used to analyze Lorentz covariant 4-tensor valued measures on Minkowski space. The graviton vacuum polarization tensor is cast as such a measure and CSR is used to obtain a well defined and finite function representing this tensor. This is then used to compute the Feynman amplitudes for examples of one-loop, two-loop and $n$-loop graviton-graviton scattering in pure gravity QG. The CSR method results in functions (densities) representing divergent Feynman integrals without requiring renormalization or counterterms.

### Probing generalized equation of state for dark energy with latest cosmological probes
Source: arXiv
Published: 2026-10-20 04:00 UTC
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23954
Source meta: preprint · primary · R4 · P5 · signal

arXiv:2610.23954v1 Announce Type: new Abstract: Baryon acoustic oscillation (BAO) measurements from DESI DR2 have strengthened indications of a time-evolving dark energy. In this paper, we investigate observational constraints on the generalized equation of state (GEoS) for dark energy, $w(a) = w_0 - w_\beta (a^{\beta}- 1)/\beta$, with latest data from UV and X-ray observations of high-redshift quasars, DESI DR2 BAO, Cosmic Microwave Background (CMB) data (including lensing) from Planck and the Atacama Cosmology Telescope (ACT), and Pantheon+ Type Ia Supernovae (SNe Ia). The full Quasar+BAO+CMB+SNe Ia data combination yields stringent constraints on the EoS parameters of $w_0=-0.840^{+0.057}_{-0.084}$, $w_\beta=-0.90^{+0.88}_{-0.23}$, and $\beta=2.2^{+2.4}_{-5.2}$. Specially, the $\beta$ parameter, which describe a broad range of dark energy evolution, is in perfect agreement with the Chevallier--Polarski--Linder model ($\beta=1$) -- the most widely used two-parameter parametrizations of dynamical dark energy.

### Two Cosmological Histories in a New Exponential Infrared $f(T)$ Teleparallel Gravity: Implications for the Hubble Tension and the Low-$\ell$ Planck TT--EE Tension
Source: arXiv
Published: 2026-10-20 04:00 UTC
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23956
Source meta: preprint · primary · R4 · P5 · signal

arXiv:2610.23956v1 Announce Type: new Abstract: We investigate a particular case of the New Exponential Infrared $f(T)$ teleparallel gravity model with the same number of free parameters as the standard $\Lambda$CDM model. Remarkably, due to the two real branches of the Lambert function $W$, the model admits two distinct cosmological solutions, denoted by $f^{(I)}$CDM and $f^{(II)}$CDM. The former exhibits a late-time phantom crossing, whereas the latter predicts an effective dark-energy density evolving from negative to positive values. Using a Markov Chain Monte Carlo analysis of the Planck CMB, DESI BAO, and Type Ia supernova data, we find that the $f^{(II)}$CDM branch develops a bimodal posterior distribution in the primordial parameters $A_s$ and $\tau_{ reio}$ for the Planck-only dataset. We show that this bimodality originates from the tension between the low-$\ell$ TT and EE likelihoods, which becomes pronounced in models exhibiting a significantly enhanced late ISW contribution relative to the $\Lambda$CDM model.

Researchers have confirmed the existence of the first microblazar. Unlike blazars, which are extragalctic and powered by supermassive black holes, microblazars are inside the Milky Way, and are powered by stellar mass black holes. The microblazar may be the source for the most energetic particles in the galaxy.

New research from the University of St Andrews has shed light on the evolution of an important yet thus far poorly understood group of marine invertebrate animals—brachiopods. The work, published in Science Advances, presents the first comprehensive phylogenomic analysis of all five extant brachiopod orders and related lineages.

### Mollusk family trees play a key role in shaping microbiomes, researchers find
Source: Phys.org Mollusks
Published: 2026-10-14 20:30 UTC
URL: https://phys.org/news/2026-10-mollusk-family-trees-play-key.html
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

A mollusk's microbial community has more to do with its family tree than its address. In other words, the composition of a mollusk's microbiome depends less on the environment in which it lives and more on what species it belongs to, say University of Michigan researchers.

### Five new species of freshwater mussels in southern US uncovered by researchers
Source: Phys.org Mollusks
Published: 2026-10-01 22:30 UTC
URL: https://phys.org/news/2026-10-species-freshwater-mussels-southern-uncovered.html
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

A new research paper published in Zootaxa describes five new species of freshwater mussels in the southern U.S., providing the scientific foundation for protecting the most imperiled group of animals in North America.

### DNA reveals chemosynthesis genes in bacteria living on coral reef fish gills
Source: Phys.org Mollusks
Published: 2026-09-27 12:00 UTC
URL: https://phys.org/news/2026-09-dna-reveals-chemosynthesis-genes-bacteria.html
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Chemosynthetic bacteria have been documented in various marine environments and in association with invertebrate hosts such as annelid worms and mollusks, but their presence in fish appears to be a new discovery. The findings show that fish gills host a complex and specialized microbiome that is not yet well understood but could play an important role in fish metabolism, immunity and health.

### Cuttlefish suckers reveal how they grip rough prey—and could inspire better technology
Source: Phys.org Mollusks
Published: 2026-09-17 11:40 UTC
URL: https://phys.org/news/2026-09-cuttlefish-suckers-reveal-rough-prey.html
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Cephalopods are a group of soft-bodied marine mollusks that include cuttlefish, octopuses, squid and nautiluses. Many of these mollusks have muscular suckers on their arms that allow them to seize prey or grip other surfaces in their surroundings.

### Pufferfish taste receptor more versatile than a human's, detecting both savory L- and sweet D-amino acids
Source: Phys.org Mollusks
Published: 2026-09-16 15:20 UTC
URL: https://phys.org/news/2026-09-pufferfish-receptor-versatile-human-savory.html
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Our sense of taste is critical for survival, alerting us to substances that provide vital nutrients and those that might poison us. Now, a team led by researchers from the University of Osaka has discovered how one such taste receptor in pufferfish can detect a surprisingly wide range of flavors at once. Their study is published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences.

### Importing a frontend Javascript library without a build system
Source: Julia Evans
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://jvns.ca/blog/2024/11/18/how-to-import-a-javascript-library/
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

I like writing Javascript without a build system and for the millionth time yesterday I ran into a problem where I needed to figure out how to import a Javascript library in my code without using a build system, and it took FOREVER to figure out how to import it because the library’s setup instructions assume that you’re using a build system. Luckily at this point I’ve mostly learned how to navigate this situation and either successfully use the library or decide it’s too difficult and switch to a different library, so here’s the guide I wish I had to importing Javascript libraries years ago. I’m only going to talk about using Javacript libraries on the frontend, and only about how to use them in a no-build-system setup.

### Why pipes sometimes get "stuck": buffering
Source: Julia Evans
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://jvns.ca/blog/2024/11/29/why-pipes-get-stuck-buffering/
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Here’s a niche terminal problem that has bothered me for years but that I never really understood until a few weeks ago. Let’s say you’re running this command to watch for some specific output in a log file: tail -f /some/log/file | grep thing1 | grep thing2 If log lines are being added to the file relatively slowly, the result I’d see is… nothing! It doesn’t matter if there were matches in the log file or not, there just wouldn’t be any output. I internalized this as “uh, I guess pipes just get stuck sometimes and don’t show me the output, that’s weird”, and I’d handle it by just running grep thing1 /some/log/file | grep thing2 instead, which would work. So as I’ve been doing a terminal deep dive over the last few months I was really excited to finally learn exactly why this happens. why this happens: buffering The reason why “pipes get stuck” sometimes is that it’s VERY common for programs to buffer their output before writing it to a pipe or file.

### "Rules" that terminal programs follow
Source: Julia Evans
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://jvns.ca/blog/2024/11/26/terminal-rules/
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Recently I’ve been thinking about how everything that happens in the terminal is some combination of: Your operating system ’s job Your shell ’s job Your terminal emulator ’s job The job of whatever program you happen to be running (like top or vim or cat ) The first three (your operating system, shell, and terminal emulator) are all kind of known quantities – if you’re using bash in GNOME Terminal on Linux, you can more or less reason about how how all of those things interact, and some of their behaviour is standardized by POSIX. But the fourth one (“whatever program you happen to be running”) feels like it could do ANYTHING. How are you supposed to know how a program is going to behave?

### What's involved in getting a "modern" terminal setup?
Source: Julia Evans
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://jvns.ca/blog/2025/01/11/getting-a-modern-terminal-setup/
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Hello! Recently I ran a terminal survey and I asked people what frustrated them. One person commented: There are so many pieces to having a modern terminal experience. I wish it all came out of the box. My immediate reaction was “oh, getting a modern terminal experience isn’t that hard, you just need to….”, but the more I thought about it, the longer the “you just need to…” list got, and I kept thinking about more and more caveats. So I thought I would write down some notes about what it means to me personally to have a “modern” terminal experience and what I think can make it hard for people to get there. what is a “modern terminal experience”? Here are a few things that are important to me, with which part of the system is responsible for them: multiline support for copy and paste : if you paste 3 commands in your shell, it should not immediately run them all! That’s scary!

### Some terminal frustrations
Source: Julia Evans
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://jvns.ca/blog/2025/02/05/some-terminal-frustrations/
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

A few weeks ago I ran a terminal survey (you can read the results here ) and at the end I asked: What’s the most frustrating thing about using the terminal for you? 1600 people answered, and I decided to spend a few days categorizing all the responses. Along the way I learned that classifying qualitative data is not easy but I gave it my best shot. I ended up building a custom tool to make it faster to categorize everything. As with all of my surveys the methodology isn’t particularly scientific. I just posted the survey to Mastodon and Twitter, ran it for a couple of days, and got answers from whoever happened to see it and felt like responding. Here are the top categories of frustrations! I think it’s worth keeping in mind while reading these comments that 40% of people answering this survey have been using the terminal for 21+ years 95% of people answering the survey have been using the terminal for at least 4 years These comments aren’t coming from total beginners. Here are the categories of frustrations!

### How to add a directory to your PATH
Source: Julia Evans
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://jvns.ca/blog/2025/02/13/how-to-add-a-directory-to-your-path/
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

I was talking to a friend about how to add a directory to your PATH today. It’s something that feels “obvious” to me since I’ve been using the terminal for a long time, but when I searched for instructions for how to do it, I actually couldn’t find something that explained all of the steps – a lot of them just said “add this to ~/.bashrc ”, but what if you’re not using bash? What if your bash config is actually in a different file? And how are you supposed to figure out which directory to add anyway? So I wanted to try to write down some more complete directions and mention some of the gotchas I’ve run into over the years. Here’s a table of contents: step 1: what shell are you using? step 2: find your shell’s config file a note on bash’s config file step 3: figure out which directory to add step 3.

### The Orchestrator's Tax
Source: Martin Fowler
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://martinfowler.com/articles/orchestrator-tax.html
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R5 · P5 · baseline

Subagents get justified by time saved and parallel execution, but Rahul Garg explains that's not what matters most. Every token in the orchestrator's context is competing for its attention, and the real value of a subagent is what it keeps out of that context. Subagents should be treated as a tool for protecting the orchestrator's working memory, offloading reasoning it doesn't need to hold onto. Doing this well means giving the orchestrator explicit ground rules for when and how to delegate. more…

### The Conductor Developer
Source: Martin Fowler
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/conductor-developer.html
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R5 · P5 · baseline

TL;DR Why I think software development is starting to feel a little more like conducting an orchestra. There’s a shift happening in software development that I don’t think we’re talking about clearly enough. For the last couple of years we’ve framed AI as a productivity tool. How much faster can it write code? How many more features can we ship? How much cheaper can we build software? I think that’s the wrong question, but I understand why. The first thing AI became good at was writing code, so naturally that’s where we focused. As AI got better at coding, I expected the bottlenecks to move through the software delivery lifecycle: from coding to design and specification, architecture, then verification. And they have. We spent a lot of time at the most recent FOSE event discussing how we ensure good design, quality and resilience while agents increasingly write the code. That’s a topic for another ramble. A few months ago, though, I realised I was looking at the wrong bottleneck. I kept assuming it would simply move to the next phase of software delivery. I was wrong.

### Fragments: August 4
Source: Martin Fowler
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-08-04.html
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R5 · P5 · baseline

There’s been a fair bit of publicity of the Open AI “rogue agent” that hacked into Hugging Face . This prompted Anthropic to check what their models were up to and, to my complete lack of surprise, discovered three incidents where models had gained unauthorized access to data in other organizations. Simon Wilison concluded : It’s abundantly clear now that running evals of cyberattack potential in models is a spectacularly risky business. Every AI lab needs to pay attention to this. Keeping a close eye on what’s happening in those sandboxes is crucial It strikes me that this is akin to a virus escaping from a laboratory. It makes clear that the model builders are not putting sufficient controls in place to prevent these lab escapes. They are morally responsible for any consequences of this, and that should extend to legal liability too. The bigger concern however is that this same kind of thing can happen with any organization running open-weight models. Lots of labs playing around with dangerous tools and little idea how to contain them.

### Fragments: August 18
Source: Martin Fowler
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-08-18.html
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R5 · P5 · baseline

Part of the reason why I’m at Thoughtworks is because I’d like to see a software development organization founded on technical excellence as an example for the rest of the industry. The trouble is that I have little aptitude or inclination for the hard work of building such an organization. So I rely on working with people who are prepared to actually put the effort in. A key partner in all of this is Rachel Laycock , who is the global CTO of Thoughtworks. Not just is she far better than me at running a technology organization, she’s also a keen observer and connector of ideas. I’ve been urging her to write these down, even if her busy schedule makes it difficult for her to compose them into something substantial. Happily she’s starting writing “Rachel’s Ramblings” Fast, imperfect, thinking out loud. Naming ideas early rather than waiting until they’re fully formed. Because the reality is, most of what I do day to day isn’t answering known questions. It’s spotting patterns and asking questions we haven’t quite figured out yet.

### AI Researchers Are Smoking Cigarettes to Prove AI Will Cure Cancer, Says Palantir Exec
Source: SoylentNews
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://soylentnews.org/article.pl?sid=26/10/19/0511219&from=rss
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

cereal_burpist writes: AI Researchers Are Smoking Cigarettes to Prove AI Will Cure Cancer, Says Palantir Exec Notes upfront: "He did not name a single researcher, cite a survey, or explain how he knows." "Palantir sells data-analysis software to governments and militaries. Most of its business relies on AI." Nothing says "I trust the future of medicine" quite like lighting a Marlboro and waiting for the chatbot to fix your lungs. That is the habit Palantir CTO Shyam Sankar says is spreading among AI researchers, who have supposedly started smoking cigarettes to prove how confident they are that AI will cure cancer. "So many AI researchers have started smoking as a way of signaling to other researchers how much they believe in AI," Sankar said in an interview on CBS News' "Face the Nation" on Sunday. "I believe so much in AI that it's going to cure cancer. So I'm showing you my chips are all in." He pointed to FTX founder Sam Bankman-Fried as the effective altruist most Americans know, calling the movement a "millenarian doomsday cult.

### Quoting Ben Affleck
Source: Simon Willison's Weblog
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://simonwillison.net/2026/Oct/7/ben-affleck/
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

I've always been kind of into computers since I was young. And then when film started to move from analog film to digital, I became more interested in that aspect of it. And the visual effects workflow for many years has included machine learning. So I can write like pretty shitty Python scripts and stuff like that because with convolutional neural networks, which were the sort of precursors to what the transformer can do, which is just much more computation simultaneously, you would do things like look at what's called a tensor, which is just the numerical translation of a visual image in numbers — like the batch number, the frame number, the red, green, and blue values of each pixel in each frame. And a tensor, you use a convolutional neural network to identify patterns in that that would reveal what's called edge detection or feature extraction, which is just identifying patterns enough to know like this is where the window ledge is, so we can more easily take the green screen image out and replace it with something. — Ben Affleck , Pythonista Tags: machine-learning , python

Release: ttok 1.0 I released ttok 0.4 , ran uv tool upgrade ttok , piped a file into the new version... and realized that it was defaulting to the GPT-4 tokenizer when it should very clearly default to GPT-5/GPT-6 instead! I figured switching the default was a reasonable excuse to finally ship a 1.0. OpenAI haven't actually confirmed that GPT-6 uses the same tokenizer as the GPT-5 family yet - there's an angry issue about it - but I found this commit by William Liu which reports on an experiment he ran confirming that the tokenizers are likely the same: All seven GPT models (5.5, 5.6 Sol/Terra/Luna, 6 Astra/Sol/Luna) report 44,794 tokens and match each other on every one of the 31 fixtures. GPT-6 introduces no input-count change on this corpus. Tags: projects , ai , openai , generative-ai , llms , tokenization

### A new feature for my blog, built using my voice
Source: Simon Willison's Weblog
Published: 2026-10-20 09:47 UTC
URL: https://simonwillison.net/2026/Oct/9/built-using-my-voice/
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

I shipped a new feature for my blog today: the Newsletters page, which offers an index of all of the newsletters I've sent out, both my free weekly Substack and my monthly sponsors-only updates. I built the feature almost entirely using my voice, chatting away to my laptop while I cooked dinner. Codex voice mode I used the ChatGPT desktop app for this, in the Codex tab, using the voice conversation mode, running against a local development environment. Here's what that looks like: I started the session against my local simonwillisonblog checkout by typing: Start dev server and open in browser This gave me a preview of the site that it would be working on, and meant that I could later ask it to show me the new pages so I could visually track its progress. Then I clicked the "Start new voice chat" button - that's not the microphone button, it's the one to the right of it - and set my laptop up in the kitchen so I could talk to it while I cooked.

### Quoting Matthew Green
Source: Simon Willison's Weblog

Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Everyone is very concerned about being respectable, so I’m going to be the goofball who raises worst-case possibilities. I think there is a 1% chance we live in Minicrypt, and a 15% chance we functionally lose confidence in our existing public-key encryption algorithms. [...] The problem here is that the speed of AI producing surprises, and the speed of human beings replacing standards (even with the very best AI assistance) are just orders of magnitude different. You only recover from a surprise like this if you do the preparation in advance. — Matthew Green , on Twitter. I looked it up and Minicrypt is Russell Impagliazzo’s hypothetical world in which public-key encryption is impossible. ### [$] Cross-crate forward declarations in Rust
Source: LWN.net
Published: 2026-10-19 13:50 UTC
URL: https://lwn.net/Articles/1099596/
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Rust has several ad-hoc mechanisms to allow user code to influence how the standard library executes. For example, custom panic handlers and custom allocators both involve registering functions that core standard library code needs to call. With more similar features planned, Jana Dönszelmann spotted the developing pattern and put together a general solution, introducing a safer, checked analogue of C's weak symbol linking. At Kangrejos 2026, she explained how this new language feature works, and proposed using it to clean up the kernel's handling of integer overflows in Rust code.

Anyway, nothing very special seems to have happened this last week, so as expected the final 7.3 is out, and thus tomorrow the merge window for 7.4 opens. Except I'm on my annual family vacation, so instead of merging anything first thing in the morning, I'll spend most of the day on a boat and under water. ### Introducing GNOME OS Preview
Source: LWN.net
Published: 2026-10-16 19:05 UTC
URL: https://lwn.net/Articles/1101057/
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

The GNOME Project has provided GNOME OS for some time; until now, the project only provided a "Nightly" edition that was built from the latest commits and was aimed solely at GNOME developers. Adrian Vovk has now announced the release of GNOME OS Preview, which is intended for a non-developer audience: GNOME OS Preview is the current GNOME OS Nightly, but without the "nightly" aspect. Instead, it's built from the latest stable GNOME release (GNOME 51.1, at the time of writing). GNOME OS Preview lives in the same branches on GitLab as the stable org.gnome.Platform Flatpak runtimes. GNOME OS Preview is only a few weeks old, so it has only seen limited real-life testing so far. We therefore expect people will uncover some issues initially. That said, Preview is significantly more stable than Nightly, which dozens of people have been daily driving without major issues for over a year. Thus, we're feeling confident that Preview is going to be reliable enough for daily use by non-developer enthusiasts.

### Building full virtual vehicles on Google Cloud: From AAOS to the smallest MCU

Imagining traditional gearheads getting their hands on software-defined vehicles is an amusing thought experiment. With their socket wrenches and air compressors at the ready, they might be confused to encounter core functions more likely to run on 1s and 0s than oil and belts. But across the auto industry these days, more and more vehicles feature prominent systems built using cloud-native development, continuous integration/continuous delivery, fast digital iteration, and plenty of AI. But there’s still a big gap to be found in many automakers’ and Tier 1 suppliers’ cloud-based development processes. On the one hand, these companies are commonly using software to develop vehicles’ digital entertainment and information screens, or other onboard high-performance computers.

### How to Use Python UDF Logging for Pipeline Observability
Source: Google Cloud Blog
Published: 2026-10-19 16:00 UTC
URL: https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/data-analytics/python-udf-logging-for-pipeline-observability-in-bigquery/

We are announcing a new logging feature that is now available in Python User-Defined Function (UDF) in BigQuery. Developers can now use Python’s standard logging library directly inside BigQuery UDFs to emit structured logs seamlessly into Cloud Logging—with zero external plugins or custom connectors required. While UDF logging enables a wide range of operational monitoring and debugging workflows, to demonstrate this feature we are showcasing an example for building fault-tolerant, resilient data pipelines. In the example below, we are capturing row-level diagnostic telemetry during query execution, so that teams can easily isolate problematic records, trigger real-time alerts, and triage issues via SQL without halting entire workloads. In this post, we’ll show you how to get started with Python UDF logging and walk through an e-commerce event ingestion pipeline as a practical, end-to-end example. Architecture overview The workflow consists of three stages: Inline validation : The Python UDF parses incoming JSON payloads.

### Developer’s guide to implementing MCP apps in Gemini
Source: Google Cloud Blog
Published: 2026-10-19 16:00 UTC
URL: https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/developers-practitioners/devs-guide-to-mcp-apps-in-gemini/
Source meta: institutional · primary · R5 · P5 · baseline

Enterprise assistants still respond mostly in text. However, that can break down for visual, multi-step work, where every adjustment costs another slow chat turn. MCP Apps ( SEP-1865 ) solves this. This open Model Context Protocol (MCP) extension pairs a tool with a live UI. In Gemini Enterprise , that UI renders directly in the chat flow inside a sandboxed iframe, and can expand to fullscreen or a side panel. Edits run locally while the workspace and assistant stay synchronized. You also avoid rebuilding core foundations: Gemini provides workforce sign-in, governed data access, and the Default Assistant, while you host your MCP server on a private Cloud Run service. Why we need MCP Apps Enterprise assistants have evolved through three generations: Generation 1 (Text chatbots): Returns static markdown with no interactive controls. Generation 2 (Standard MCP tools): Invokes live APIs, but outputs static text. Every change requires another model turn. Generation 3 (Interactive MCP Apps): Pairs each tool with a live workspace.

### Who judges the judge? Three helpful ways to audit your LLM evaluators
Source: Google Cloud Blog
Published: 2026-10-19 16:00 UTC
URL: https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/consulting/three-ways-to-audit-your-llm-evaluators/
Source meta: institutional · primary · R5 · P5 · baseline

Is your LLM-as-a-judge truly objective? There’s a common misconception in tech that humans are subjective, so we should rely on AI for objective evaluation. But the reality is that LLMs are also subjective. Just like human reviewers, they can carry biases: Verbosity bias : LLM judges usually favor longer, confident-sounding answers , even when the underlying facts are wrong. Position bias : Swapping the order of two options can cause an LLM judge to flip its verdict over 75% of the time . The halo effect : Because LLMs generate text piece by piece, clean grammar and formatting can trick an AI judge into giving top marks for accuracy while missing errors. Agreeableness bias : LLM judges are great at recognizing valid work. But with detecting errors, they may fail to catch incorrect, buggy, or unsafe outputs up to 75% of the time . Self-enhancement bias: Also known as egocentric bias, AI evaluators systematically overrate outputs that were generated by themselves or from their own family. Ultimately, LLM judges are not objective arbiters of truth.

By Kevin Zielnicki, Guy Aridor, Aurélien Bibaut, Allen Tran, Winston Chou, and Nathan Kallus Introduction Personalization is everywhere on the internet. It shapes the products we discover, the stories we read, the music we hear, and the movies and TV shows we watch. At Netflix, personalization has been central to the member experience for decades — from the Netflix Prize to our modern recommender system that helps members navigate a large and constantly changing catalog. But personalization is not only an algorithmic problem, it is also a measurement problem. How do we know how much value personalization creates? More precisely, how do we separate the appeal of a title from the value created by recommending that title to the right member at the right time? In a new article in the International Journal of Industrial Organization — which we are also presenting at this year’s ACM Conference on Economics & Computation (EC ‘26) — we develop a model of member behavior, title choice, and personalized recommendations.

Source: Netflix TechBlog

Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R5 · P5 · baseline

By Oleksii Tkachuk , Rajiv Shringi , Chris Lohfink , Abe Ratnofsky , Kartik Sathyanarayanan Introduction In an earlier post , we introduced the TimeSeries Abstraction, our high-throughput, low-latency platform designed to store and query temporal event data at scale. It has since become the operational backbone for Netflix’s largest datasets, which now span multiple petabytes. However, managing such large datasets in hot storage, tuned for low-millisecond latency access , is not cheap. At Netflix, cost-efficiency is a continuous discipline rather than a temporary initiative. This post shares two solutions that significantly reduce storage costs by continuously moving older, less frequently accessed data into cheaper object storage: Solution 1 : Uses Apache Kafka and Flink to stream data directly to S3 Solution 2 : Leverages Cassandra backups in S3 and pluggable Memtables to read data directly from S3 We’ll start with the Kafka/Flink-based design, then describe how we evolved to a native Cassandra-based cold tier.

By Yogesh Nagarur Introduction In Part 1 of this series , we shared how Netflix built the Ads Event pipeline and approached it as a system design problem: replacing fragmented pipelines with a centralized collection, enrichment, and a standard data contract. The result was Ads Event Publisher, the system that collects ad telemetry from client devices, enriches it with the context of the ad that was actually served, and publishes a single, unified stream of ad events to every downstream consumer: frequency capping, metrics, billing, measurement partners, and reporting. Any member viewing or account details that move through this pipeline end-to-end are encrypted and handled in accordance with Netflix’s strict privacy and security practices to safeguard member PII. If ad serving is the “brain” and ad events are the “heartbeats” that feed it, then enrichment is what gives each heartbeat its meaning, turning a bare “[Somebody] watched an ad to completion” signal into “ this campaign, this creative, at this price, for this break.

### Why Is My Kubernetes Job Still Pending? Tracing Kueue Admission with Headlamp
Source: Kubernetes Blog
Published: 2026-10-14 18:35 UTC
URL: https://kubernetes.io/blog/2026/10/14/tracing-kueue-admission-with-headlamp/
Source meta: institutional · primary · R5 · P5 · baseline

You submit a Kubernetes Job and wait for its Pods to appear. A minute passes. Then another. The Job is still suspended, there are no application logs, and there may not even be a Pod to inspect. In a cluster managed by Kueue , this can be completely normal. Kueue may still be deciding whether the Job can use the requested resources. The useful question is not only “Why is the Pod pending?” It is first “Has Kueue admitted the Workload?” The Kueue plugin for Headlamp brings that investigation into the Headlamp UI. Instead of reconstructing the admission path from several commands and large YAML objects, you can begin with the Workload, read its condition message, and follow links through the LocalQueue, ClusterQueue, ResourceFlavor, and Cohort. This article follows that Headlamp-first path. The command line appears only in a short optional section for reproducing the example. Before you begin You need a Kubernetes cluster with Kueue installed , a Headlamp installation , and the 0.1.0-alpha Kueue plugin .

### The Shift to cgroup v2 in Kubernetes: What You Need to Know
Source: Kubernetes Blog
Published: 2026-10-06 18:00 UTC
URL: https://kubernetes.io/blog/2026/10/06/kubernetes-cgroups-v2-shift/
Source meta: institutional · primary · R5 · P5 · baseline

In Linux, cgroups (control groups) are a kernel feature used for managing system resources. Kubernetes uses cgroups to allocate resources like CPU and memory to containers, ensuring that applications run smoothly without interfering with each other. With the release of Kubernetes v1.31, support for v1 cgroup management moved into maintenance mode . Support for v2 cgroup management has been stable since Kubernetes v1.25. Compared with cgroup v1, cgroup v2 provides a single unified hierarchy, a more consistent interface, and a stronger foundation for resource isolation and modern resource-management features. Deprecation of cgroup v1 Kubernetes has deprecated cgroup v1 . Starting with Kubernetes v1.35, failCgroupV1 defaults to true , so the kubelet does not start on a cgroup v1 node by default. Administrators can temporarily set failCgroupV1: false in the kubelet configuration file , but removal will follow the Kubernetes deprecation policy . Further removal work is tracked in KEP-5573: Remove cgroup v1 support . If you are still on a release older than v1.

### Scaling Kubernetes Workloads with Node Swap
Source: Kubernetes Blog
Published: 2026-10-05 18:00 UTC
URL: https://kubernetes.io/blog/2026/10/05/scaling-kubernetes-workloads-with-node-swap/
Source meta: institutional · primary · R5 · P5 · baseline

Memory is often the first hard limit a Kubernetes cluster hits. Nodes run out of RAM long before they run out of CPU, and the new wave of agentic AI workloads makes this worse. These workloads demand large memory footprints to start up and run untrusted code, then sit idle waiting for the next prompt. That idle but resident memory is expensive, and it caps how many pods a node can hold. This is where swap helps. Kubernetes support for running nodes with swap enabled reached General Availability in v1.34, and by backing that swap with fast NVMe solid state drives (SSDs), a node can page out dormant memory and pack in far more pods. This post explains how we benchmarked that approach across three workloads, including CI/CD kernel builds, sandboxed headless browsers, and isolated Python runtimes; we found density gains of up to 3×, often with little or no latency cost.

### Kubernetes v1.37: Tracking When a PersistentVolumeClaim Was Last Used (Beta)
Source: Kubernetes Blog
Published: 2026-09-21 18:30 UTC
URL: https://kubernetes.io/blog/2026/09/21/kubernetes-v1-37-pvc-last-used-time/
Source meta: institutional · primary · R5 · P5 · baseline

Kubernetes v1.37 promotes the PersistentVolumeClaimUnusedSinceTime feature gate to Beta (enabled by default). With this feature, the PersistentVolumeClaim (PVC) protection controller adds an Unused condition to each PVC, telling you whether any running pod currently references it — no custom tooling or cross-referencing required. For the API definition of PVC conditions, see the PersistentVolumeClaim API reference . Read on to learn how the Unused condition works and how to use it. Why track PVC usage? In large-scale Kubernetes clusters, it is common for users to create PVCs and then delete the associated pods without cleaning up the storage, because Kubernetes does not automatically delete PVCs when their pods are removed (to protect against accidental data loss). Over time, these orphaned PVCs may accumulate, silently consuming storage capacity and driving up cloud costs. Before Kubernetes v1.37, it was easy to identify an unused PersistentVolume, but much harder to determine whether a PVC was still being used.

Kubernetes v1.37 brings important storage security features: emptyDir permission modes and bind mount options. They help application programmers and security professionals implement rigorous security policies, for example, prohibiting deletion of files across containers or execution of arbitrary binaries from writable volumes, directly in Kubernetes without any complicated circumvention. Linux storage and permission fundamentals Before diving into the new Kubernetes features, let us briefly review the low-level Linux security mechanisms that make them possible. Bind mount flags When Linux mounts or remounts a directory, Virtual File System (VFS) flags control what actions are permitted on that filesystem: noexec : Do not permit direct execution of any binaries on the mounted filesystem. nosuid : Do not allow set-user-identifier or set-group-identifier bits to take effect. nodev : Do not interpret character or block special devices on the file system. ### The Robot I Didn’t Expect to Build
Source: IEEE Spectrum Robotics
Published: 2026-10-19 13:00 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/agility-robotics-digit-5-redesign
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Agility Robotics , the company I cofounded out of my robotics lab at Oregon State University in 2015, has just launched Digit 5 . It’s designed to be the first humanoid robot that can safely and economically do useful work at scale, allowing us to bring tens of thousands of humanoid workers into the real world. Engineered to lift 23-kilogram bins onto high shelves and with a battery that can run for 90 minutes on a 9-minute charge, Digit 5’s goal is to handle heavy warehouse tasks shift after shift, filling the need for labor in jobs that are too physically stressful or too dull for humans to want to do. Digit 5 is what Agility has been working toward for over a decade. But the robot is heavy and bulky, lacking the graceful natural motion of its predecessors, and it’s farther than I thought possible from the robot I dreamed of building when I started this company. To deliver a useful commercial product, Agility has taken a path that is very different from what I first imagined.

### Hera Mission Prepares for Follow-Up Asteroid Imaging
Source: IEEE Spectrum Aerospace
Published: 2026-10-18 13:00 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/esa-hera-dimorphos-rendezvous
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

In one month, the European Space Agency’s Hera mission will make history. Moving at 12 kilometers per second relative to Earth, the mission is about to rendezvous with the binary Dimorphos-Didymos asteroid pair. NASA’s Double Asteroid Redirection Test (DART) mission slammed into Dimorphos in 2022, and now Hera will provide a follow-up view of the mission’s resulting handiwork. The European Space Agency (ESA) recently released a thought-provoking look at the perspectives of Hera and what an astronaut might see from the surface of the asteroids as the mission speeds by. But what would it be like to hitch a ride aboard Hera? Launched on a SpaceX Falcon 9 rocket from Cape Canaveral, Fla., on 7 October 2024, Hera is ESA’s first Space Safety Programme-class mission. The spacecraft took just over two years to reach its destination. Even now, the pair of asteroids is little more than a few pixels across in Hera’s view. The mission imaged Didymos in July 2026.

### Q&A: Mars Perseverance Rover Sets a Distance Record
Source: IEEE Spectrum Robotics
Published: 2026-10-13 15:02 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/mars-rover-distance-record
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

On 11 October , the Perseverance rover completed a drive that brought its total distance traveled on Mars to 28.10 miles (45.22 kilometers). This is a new record for Mars rovers, enabled in large part by Perseverance ’s ability to drive with much more autonomy than its predecessors. Over the past five years, Perseverance ’s autonomy software has been steadily upgraded, and to learn more about how the record was broken and what the future holds for the rover, we spoke with Mark Maimone , the Perseverance Robotic Operations Deputy Team Chief at NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory, who designed and developed the self-driving software for Mars rovers Spirit, Opportunity, Curiosity, and Perseverance. Before we talk about Perseverance, let’s pour one out for the previous record holder, Opportunity , which landed on Mars in January 2004 for a three-month mission and ended its epic journey 15 years later in February 2019.

### NASA Wants More Than 70 Moon Landings in the Next Decade—But How?
Source: IEEE Spectrum Aerospace
Published: 2026-10-12 15:27 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/nasa-moon-base-landings
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

At Firefly Aerospace in Texas, engineers have been doing what NASA’s chief, Jared Isaacman, likes to call “the near-impossible.” The Firefly team has been assembling Blue Ghost Mission 2 , a robotic spacecraft intended to land on the far side of the moon in 2027. No U.S. spacecraft has ever gone there. Not very many have landed on the near side, either, for that matter. Blue Ghost 2 has thousands of potential failure points, and there is almost no room for error. Two ships sent by a competitor, Houston-based Intuitive Machines , reached the moon’s near side in 2024 and 2025 but fell over on landing and returned little useful data. In 2024, another mission, Peregrine Mission One , by Pittsburgh-based Astrobotic Technology , missed the moon entirely because a single helium valve wouldn’t seat properly. In summary: Moon landings are supremely hard. So far, since Project Apollo ended in 1972, U.S. spacecraft have landed intact exactly…once.

### Lunar Mission Will Explore Old Lava Tubes as Potential Habitats
Source: IEEE Spectrum Aerospace
Published: 2026-10-11 13:00 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/nasa-moon-mission-gimli-artemis
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

The chief aim of NASA’s Artemis Program is to create a “sustained program of lunar exploration and development.” Beyond returning astronauts to the moon for the first time since the Apollo era , NASA and other space agencies hope to build the infrastructure that will enable regular, long-duration missions to the lunar surface. In addition to targeting the South Pole-Aitken Basin , NASA is eyeing lunar caves as a potential site for habitats and other lunar facilities. Meanwhile, space agencies need to send large amounts of cargo and robotic explorers to the surface. This includes the Geophysical Instruments for Marius Lunar pit Investigation (GIMLI), a Planetary Science Institute –led proposal that will investigate a “skylight,” or a hole in an old lunar lava tube, to see if a large cave system extends beyond it . Such a location could provide a safe place for astronauts to operate on the surface in the future. The mission team will be led by Than Putzig , an associate director and senior scientist at PSI.

### This Disembodied Hand Is All the Robot You Need
Source: IEEE Spectrum Robotics
Published: 2026-10-08 12:00 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/walking-robotic-hand
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

A hand attached to the end of an arm is a given for most humans, but what if robots didn’t have to play by the same rules as we do? Researchers from ETH Zurich have turned an off-the-shelf robotic hand into a completely self-contained robot that walks on its fingertips while using those same fingers to manipulate its environment. Besides making the perfect Halloween accessory for anyone planning to dress up as a member of the Addams Family—who were famously accompanied by a disembodied walking hand called Thing —this robotic hand could make it possible to operate controls and handle objects in places a traditional robot arm can’t reach, the researchers say. Soft Robotics Lab, ETH Zurich/YouTube Amirhossein Kazemipour , a Ph.D. candidate in the Soft Robotics Lab at ETH Zurich who led the project, believes this hand could prove useful in contexts like fiddly maintenance tasks in industrial settings, or in search and rescue, where a robot could detach its hand to explore confined spaces too tight for the rest of its body.

### HiPHI: A Large-Scale Benchmark for High-Precision Human Motion and Object Interaction
Source: IEEE Spectrum Robotics
Published: 2026-10-07 13:49 UTC
URL: https://content.knowledgehub.wiley.com/hiphi-a-large-scale-benchmark-for-high-precision-human-motion-and-object-interaction/
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

This White Paper gives robotics researchers and engineers an overview of a new large-scale motion capture dataset built to close the data gap limiting humanoid robot learning. It also shows how policies trained on the dataset transfer to a real humanoid robot. What you will learn about: Why humanoid robot learning, a central problem in embodied AI and Physical AI, needs data that internet video and existing motion capture datasets cannot provide. How FrameNet , a linguistic framework for human action, can guide motion capture collection to systematically cover a broad range of whole-body motion. Why synchronized object trajectories and meshes make human-object interaction data useful for teaching robots real-world tasks such as carrying, pushing, and pulling. How reinforcement learning policies trained on this motion capture data improve with scale, and how sim-to-real transfer carries them onto a physical humanoid robot. Download this free whitepaper now!

### Ukraine’s Air Defenses Have No Answer for Russia’s New Jet Drones
Source: IEEE Spectrum Aerospace
Published: 2026-09-30 11:00 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/jet-powered-shahed-drone
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Since the discovery of the first remnants of a jet-powered Shahed drone near Kyiv in June 2025, these upgraded kamikaze UAVs have become a nightmare for Ukrainian civilians. Described by some analysts as the most significant Russian drone innovation since the beginning of the war, these uncrewed aerial vehicles are outrunning the beleaguered nation’s innovative interceptor systems, forcing a pivot in technology development and air-defense strategies. Russia manufactures the Iran-designed long-range Shahed drones , under the name Geran at its Yelabuga factory in Tatarstan, some 800 kilometers east of Moscow. In a little over a year, Russian engineers have developed three iterations of the jet-powered killer drone— Geran-3 , 4, and 5. Geran-3 , although fitted with a Chinese-made Telefly JT80 turbojet engine that enabled it to reach speeds of up to 370 kilometers per hour, was not very maneuverable and for that reason not much more effective than the propeller-powered Geran-2 .

### Generative AI Gives Spacecraft the Autonomy Engineers Once Feared
Source: IEEE Spectrum Aerospace
Published: 2026-09-28 11:00 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/generative-ai-in-space-exploration
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Space was always supposed to be the final frontier of human exploration. It’s shaping up to be the final frontier for artificial intelligence too. Last December, NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory used Anthropic’s Claude models to help plan two Mars drives for the Perseverance rover , with human planners checking and adjusting the route before upload. In May, NASA and IBM put a compressed AI model on the International Space Station and a satellite to identify things like floods and clouds from orbit, the first model of its kind demonstrated in space. And in July, astronauts on the ISS tested a large language model to see if it could help with questions on maintenance procedures . These experiments point to a larger shift in space engineering. For decades, engineers on Earth determined what a machine in space would do, and the machine would do exactly that. Now, researchers are testing whether nondeterministic systems like generative AI can give spacecraft more flexibility to interpret their surroundings, plan tasks, and one day make decisions for themselves.

### Spain’s First Astronaut, Pedro Duque, Named IEEE Honorary Member
Source: IEEE Spectrum Aerospace
Published: 2026-09-22 18:00 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/spain-astronaut-ieee-honorary-member
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Many youngsters fascinated by exploring outer space dream of becoming an astronaut, but few do. One who had the right stuff is Pedro Duque , who was Spain’s first astronaut. The aeronautics engineer flew aboard the space shuttle Discovery and the International Space Station . After retiring as an astronaut, he headed Spain’s Ministry of Science, Innovation, and Universities . Today he is the chairman of HispaSat , a Spanish satellite company. Pedro Duque Employer HispaSat in Madrid Title President and chairman of the board Member grade Honorary member Alma mater The Polytechnic University of Madrid Nearly 30 years after his first mission, Duque is still Spain’s most famous astronaut. Three public schools have been named after him, and he has received numerous awards. This year, the IEEE Board of Directors made him an IEEE honorary member for “contributions to space exploration, leadership in collaborative science and technology programs, and serving as a role model for younger generations.

Earlier this month, we saw how the humble ESP32-S3 could be turned into an SDR transmitter and receiver; then we saw developer h0m3us3r release the w1700k-sdr project, which turns a Gemtek W1700K Wi-Fi 7 router into a wideband SDR receiver. The LR2021-SDR open-source project by Samuel Reynolds (SAMS0N1TE) follows the sample principles and turns the Semtech LR2021 LoRa transceiver into a software-defined radio (SDR) capable of receiving signals from 100 MHz to 2.74 GHz. Using an undocumented command in the chip’s ROM, this project exposes raw 16-bit I/Q samples, allowing software to decode P25 Phase 1 digital voice, POCSAG pager messages, and analog FM signals without an external SDR dongle. LR2021-SDR firmware features: Supported hardware – Semtech LR2021 running ROM 1.24; not compatible with the SX1262 variant for raw I/Q access. ### The Robot I Didn’t Expect to Build
Source: IEEE Spectrum
Published: 2026-10-19 13:00 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/agility-robotics-digit-5-redesign
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Agility Robotics , the company I cofounded out of my robotics lab at Oregon State University in 2015, has just launched Digit 5 . It’s designed to be the first humanoid robot that can safely and economically do useful work at scale, allowing us to bring tens of thousands of humanoid workers into the real world. Engineered to lift 23-kilogram bins onto high shelves and with a battery that can run for 90 minutes on a 9-minute charge, Digit 5’s goal is to handle heavy warehouse tasks shift after shift, filling the need for labor in jobs that are too physically stressful or too dull for humans to want to do. Digit 5 is what Agility has been working toward for over a decade. But the robot is heavy and bulky, lacking the graceful natural motion of its predecessors, and it’s farther than I thought possible from the robot I dreamed of building when I started this company. To deliver a useful commercial product, Agility has taken a path that is very different from what I first imagined.

### This Hidden Device Could Free Up Gigawatts of Grid Power
Source: IEEE Spectrum
Published: 2026-10-17 13:00 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/variable-frequency-drives
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

This article was originally published by Canary Media . America’s energy supply crunch is driving a surge of federal and private investment in new electricity generation — from polluting gas turbines to cleaner developments like solar farms and geothermal plants . But gigawatts of power are already available for the taking across the U.S. economy in the form of wasted energy from an unexpected source: motors. Some 50 million electric motors drive the fans, pumps, and compressors found in virtually every factory and building. Together, they gobble enough electricity to equal nearly a third of total U.S. power demand. Often, the motors use far more energy than they actually need to keep America’s machines humming. Companies can install devices known as variable frequency drives, or VFDs, to make motors run super efficiently. Yet adoption remains relatively low , in part because of the perceived hassle of retrofitting equipment and because they’re typically not required .

Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Seeed Studio has introduced the XIAO IPS touch display series, a family of six compact development boards built around the XIAO ESP32-S3 Plus or XIAO nRF52840 Plus MCU, with 0.96-inch, 1.14-inch, and 1.47-inch IPS color displays. All six boards include a 6-axis IMU, a PDM digital microphone, user buttons, and LiPo battery support. The larger 1.47-inch version adds capacitive touch and a microSD card slot, whereas the 1.14-inch model includes a Grove I2C connector for connecting external sensors. The ESP32-S3 versions support Wi-Fi and Bluetooth LE connectivity, while the nRF52840 models focus on lower-power Bluetooth LE applications. The boards are designed for portable dashboards, smart home controllers, wearable devices, sensor monitoring, and other small IoT projects. ### IEEE Offers More Services to Cities That Want to Be Smarter
Source: IEEE Spectrum
Published: 2026-10-16 18:00 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/ieee-smart-cities-new-services
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

More and more city governments are using advanced technologies to improve livability for their residents. Municipalities use data from smartphones and sensors for traffic enforcement, traffic control, and waste management. They also have implemented artificial intelligence and deep learning programs. Dubai , Paris , and Coral Gables , Fla., are among the cities that have invested heavily in programs to become smarter. But along with the conveniences that come with a smart city transition, there have been challenges including safety and privacy issues and eroding relationships between residents and city officials. Although many initiatives have delivered real innovations, not all have been successful. Inefficient global coordination has hindered projects with unclear standards, socioeconomic differences, and a lack of worldwide governance, leading to a disconnect between the technologies and the ability to adapt to them. Citizens’ trust in the technologies suffers because of privacy breaches, personal data leaks, and other issues.

### Simulation App Speeds Up Electric Truck Battery Design
Source: IEEE Spectrum
Published: 2026-10-16 16:49 UTC
URL: https://spectrum.ieee.org/electric-truck-battery-design-simulation
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

This article is brought to you by COMSOL . These days, no one blinks an eye at two-day, one-day, and even same-day package deliveries. Despite the plethora of localized fulfillment and distributi on centers that have cropped up to meet the demand, delivery trucks still need to make considerably long drives, often over highways. To reduce fuel and maintenance costs as well as carbon emissions, companies are investing in electric trucks for their delivery fleets. While turning to electric trucks is a promising solution, the battery packs incorporated into these automotive designs often fall short in the lifetime needed to make long-distance highway travel feasible. To help overcome the issue of battery lifetime, a consortium of universities and industry partners incorporated multiscale simulation and a custom simulation app into the battery manufacturing process.

### How Technology Empowers—and Imperils—Dictators
Source: Schneier on Security
Published: 2026-10-20 09:48 UTC
URL: https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/10/how-technology-empowers-and-imperils-dictators.html
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R5 · P5 · baseline

This essay was written with Seva Gunitsky, and originally appeared in Foreign Affairs . Two weeks after Moscow’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine in March 2022, the Russian TV Channel One editor Marina Ovsyannikova burst onto the set of the evening newscast. She held up a hand-drawn sign behind the anchor’s head that read: “Stop the war. Don’t believe propaganda. They’re lying to you!” She shouted, “No to war!” until she was dragged away. No one has protested the war on Russian television since then, partly as a result of tighter security and a general climate of fear. But in October 2024, Margarita Simonyan, one of Russia’s chief propagandists, gave another explanation. A growing number of the RT network’s anchors, she explained to an interviewer, are not real. “That face doesn’t exist. We generated the voice and everything else.” They were, she meant, produced by artificial intelligence. In a follow-up interview with the newspaper ...

### Friday Squid Blogging: I Caught a Squid
Source: Schneier on Security
Published: 2026-10-20 09:48 UTC
URL: https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/10/friday-squid-blogging-i-caught-a-squid.html
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R5 · P5 · baseline

On Wednesday I spent a day fishing, on a small boat out of Gloucester, MA. We caught many cod (none of which we could keep), and a bunch of hake and mackerel (all of which we could keep). And…I caught a squid! Near as I can tell, it’s a longfin squid, sometimes called a Boston squid ( Doryteuthis (Amerigo) pealeii ). That night I cooked it over a barbecue grill—hot and fast. Delicious.

### Require Police to Get a Warrant to Search ALPR Data
Source: Electronic Frontier Foundation
Published: 2026-10-19 23:28 UTC
URL: https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/require-police-get-warrant-search-alpr-data
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

This post was co-written by Andi Cao , an EFF legal intern. Automated license plate readers (ALPRs) are mass surveillance. They photograph every passing car and log where and when it was captured, building a searchable record of where nearly everyone drives. Cities should not buy them , and cities that already have them should shut them down. Where these systems still operate, courts and state legislatures must reduce their harms by (among other things) requiring police to get a warrant before searching stored ALPR data. A warrant requirement would not make mass surveillance acceptable, but it would require police to justify a search to a judge before accessing records of people’s movements. Without this independent judicial check on police power, officers have been searching this data for improper reasons (many exposed by EFF) that violate our fundamental rights. A warrant requires a judge to find probable cause and specificity—many warrantless police searches of ALPR data lack both.

### Announcing Privacy Badger for Safari on macOS
Source: Electronic Frontier Foundation
Published: 2026-10-19 17:59 UTC
URL: https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/announcing-privacy-badger-safari-macos
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Exciting news for Safari users: Privacy Badger is now available on Safari for macOS! You’ll need Safari version 26.5 or higher to use it. Whether you want to protect yourself from data brokers or just don’t want Big Tech profiting off your browsing data, Privacy Badger is here to help. More than 4 million people already use EFF’s free tracker-blocking extension to take a stand against online surveillance. Safari users can now join them and EFF in the fight for a better web. Install Privacy Badger Support the Fight Against Online Tracking Safari support has been one of Privacy Badger’s most requested features for years. Thanks to recent improvements in Safari's extension support, we’ve finally made it happen for Safari version 26.5 and higher. Privacy Badger is now available on all major desktop browsers. And before you ask, yes, we will look into supporting Safari for iOS once we've ironed out any issues on macOS. Why Install Privacy Badger on Safari? Most websites contain invisible tracking code that lets companies collect, share, and profit from your personal data.

### EFF Launches Privacy Badger for Safari on macOS
Source: Electronic Frontier Foundation
Published: 2026-10-19 16:00 UTC
URL: https://www.eff.org/press/releases/eff-launches-privacy-badger-safari-macos
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Free Tracker Blocker Used by Millions Will Protect Even More Users' Privacy SAN FRANCISCO—Privacy Badger, the award-winning tracker blocker made by the member-supported digital rights nonprofit Electronic Frontier Foundation (EFF), is now available for Safari on macOS. Safari users can download the free browser extension today and join the more than 4 million people using Privacy Badger to take a stand against data brokers and online surveillance. Users will need Safari version 26.5 or higher to use it. Ranked as one of the best ad tracker blockers by The Wirecutter , Consumer Reports , PC Mag and more, Privacy Badger is different from other blocking extensions. Created by EFF to stop companies from spying on you online, Privacy Badger automatically learns to block hidden trackers on the web, along with additional privacy-protecting features. "Most websites contain invisible tracking code that lets companies collect, share, and profit from your personal data," said Lena Cohen, Staff Technologist at EFF.

### In Memoriam: Barry Steinhardt
Source: Electronic Frontier Foundation
Published: 2026-10-16 00:07 UTC
URL: https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/memoriam-barry-steinhardt
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Barry Steinhardt, a lifelong privacy champion and an early leader in the battle for digital civil liberties who served as EFF’s president in 1998-99, died last week at age 73. A Connecticut native, Steinhardt led the Vermont and Pennsylvania affiliates of the American Civil Liberties Union before becoming associate director of the national ACLU. There, he helped spearhead the fight to overturn much of the Communications Decency Act, resulting in the U.S. Supreme Court’s landmark Reno v. ACLU ruling in 1997. When the internet was still young, Steinhardt became one of the first attorneys to specialize in the intersection of technology and privacy . In early 1998 he took a leave of absence from the ACLU to become EFF’s president, and then returned to the ACLU in 1999. In 2002 he founded the ACLU’s Program on Technology and Liberty. After retiring from the ACLU, he continued on as a senior advisor to London-based Privacy International .

Source: Electronic Frontier Foundation
Published: 2026-10-15 20:08 UTC
URL: https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/our-lawsuit-against-trump-administrations-social-media-surveillance-moves-forward
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

No one should have to choose between speaking freely and protecting their immigration status. Yet that’s precisely the choice the Trump administration’s mass surveillance of online speech forces on noncitizens living in the U.S. Last fall, we sued on behalf of three major labor unions to stop this surveillance program. And just this month, we won our first major ruling: A federal district court denied the government’s motion to dismiss our lawsuit. This means the case can move forward—a win for free speech and a critical step in protecting our First Amendment rights online. We’ve already explained why this is a big deal for our clients, so let's take a closer look at the court’s reasoning. Our Lawsuit Since taking power, the Trump administration has used AI and other automated tools to broadly surveil the social media and online speech of visa and green card holders, with the goal of identifying and punishing people who express views it opposes.

### We’re Asking the Wisconsin Supreme Court to End Warrantless ALPR Searches
Source: Electronic Frontier Foundation
Published: 2026-10-14 23:21 UTC
URL: https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/were-asking-wisconsin-supreme-court-end-warrantless-alpr-searches
Source meta: specialist_media · specialist · R4 · P4 · baseline

Today, EFF joined the ACLU of Wisconsin in filing a petition to the Wisconsin Supreme Court, asking it to rule that the state’s constitution requires a warrant for retrospective searches of ALPR databases used by law enforcement in four cities: Milwaukee, Racine, Kenosha, and Green Bay. We represent a religious organization, the Wisconsin Council of Churches, as well as an individual pastor in Green Bay. As the petition explains: Right now, Automated License Plate Readers (“ALPRs”) are recording the movements of vast numbers of Wisconsin drivers as they seek medical care, attend 12-step recovery meetings, attend religious services, participate in protests, attend political meetings, and engage in many other private, sensitive activities. Law enforcement entities throughout Wisconsin collect, store, share, and aggregate this ALPR data. Then they search it, retrospectively tracking the movements of individuals and entire communities in ways that would have been prohibitively costly, if not outright impossible, before the advent of ALPR technology.
